Table 4
Deep Learning
1. Q: What is deep learning, and why has it become a major area of artificial intelligence?
A: Show answer
Deep learning is a subfield of machine learning that uses neural networks with multiple processing layers to learn patterns and representations from data. Instead of requiring people to specify every relevant feature manually, a deep learning model can learn increasingly complex representations during training. For example, an image model may learn simple visual patterns in early layers and combine them into shapes, objects, or more abstract features in later layers. Deep learning can be particularly effective for images, audio, text, sequences, and other complex data. Its success has been supported by the growth of digital datasets, improved algorithms, and powerful computing hardware, especially GPUs and specialized accelerators. However, deep learning is not automatically superior to every other approach. Its suitability depends on the quality and quantity of available data, the difficulty of the task, computational resources, evaluation evidence, and the consequences of incorrect predictions.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the relationship between artificial intelligence, machine learning, and deep learning, and identify a problem for which learned representations could be valuable."

Key point:
- Deep learning learns complex representations through multiple neural network layers.
- Its effectiveness depends on data, model design, computational resources, and evaluation quality.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
2. Q: Why was ImageNet important to the development of deep learning?
A: Show answer
ImageNet helped demonstrate the value of large, organized image datasets for training and evaluating computer vision models. Its images are associated with categories that enable supervised learning, while standardized benchmarks make it possible to compare models on a common task. The ImageNet Large Scale Visual Recognition Challenge, particularly its 2012 edition, became an influential milestone when AlexNet demonstrated substantial improvements in image classification using a deep convolutional neural network. The significance was not simply that researchers had more images. Large datasets, accessible computing power, effective optimization, and suitable network architectures worked together to improve performance. ImageNet also helped establish benchmarking as a means of measuring progress. Nevertheless, benchmark success does not guarantee equal performance on other datasets, different populations, unfamiliar environments, or operational tasks. Reported accuracy and error rates must always be interpreted in the context of the dataset, task definition, evaluation method, and historical period.

Study instruction:
- "Review the role of ImageNet in computer vision and distinguish benchmark performance from performance in a real-world deployment."

Key point:
- Large labelled datasets and standardized evaluation helped accelerate computer vision research.
- Historical benchmark results should not be treated as universal measures of model accuracy.

Source links:
- ImageNet — Image Database and Research Resource
- Keras — Computer Vision Examples
3. Q: How does deep learning differ from traditional machine learning?
A: Show answer
Traditional machine learning often relies on features selected or engineered by people, although some algorithms can learn useful representations automatically. For example, a conventional image classifier might be supplied with measurements describing edges, shapes, textures, or other manually selected characteristics. Deep learning can learn hierarchical features directly from image pixels when sufficient suitable training data and computational resources are available. This can reduce the need for manual feature engineering, especially when the relationships in the data are difficult to describe explicitly. The distinction is not absolute: traditional machine learning can use learned features, and deep learning still requires human decisions about data preparation, labels, architectures, objectives, and validation. Traditional models may be more practical for small tabular datasets, limited computing environments, or problems where interpretability is especially important. Model selection should therefore be based on evidence and constraints rather than the assumption that deeper networks are always better.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a feature-engineered machine learning solution with a deep learning solution for the same image classification problem."

Key point:
- Deep learning can learn features automatically through multiple layers.
- The simpler approach may be preferable when it meets the task's accuracy, cost, and interpretability requirements.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
4. Q: What is an artificial neural network, and what are its basic components?
A: Show answer
An artificial neural network is a computational model composed of connected units, often called neurons or nodes, arranged in layers. The input layer receives the information being processed. Hidden layers transform intermediate representations, and the output layer produces predictions or other results. Connections between units have trainable weights, while units or transformations may include biases. Activation functions introduce nonlinear transformations that allow networks to represent relationships more complex than a purely linear model can represent. During training, an optimization process adjusts model parameters to reduce a defined loss function. A network's actual behaviour depends on its architecture, input representation, learned parameter values, activation functions, training data, and objective. The biological analogy is useful for introducing the idea of interconnected processing units, but artificial neural networks should not be treated as literal copies of the human brain or assumed to reproduce biological cognition.

Study instruction:
- "Draw a simple neural network and label its input layer, hidden layers, output layer, weights, biases, and activation functions."

Key point:
- Neural networks learn through parameterized transformations across connected layers.
- Their architecture and training process jointly determine predictive behaviour.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- Google for Developers — Neural Network Activation Functions
5. Q: What roles do weights and biases play in a neural network?
A: Show answer
Weights determine how strongly input values contribute to subsequent calculations. A weight can be positive or negative and can increase or decrease a unit's pre-activation value. Biases provide additional adjustable offsets, allowing a unit to change its activation threshold independently of the input values. A simplified neuron calculates a weighted sum of inputs, adds a bias, and passes the result through an activation function. During training, the optimizer updates weights and biases using information about prediction errors and their gradients. These parameters are learned rather than usually prescribed as final values by the developer. Their numerical magnitudes should not automatically be interpreted as causal importance or a complete explanation of the model's reasoning, especially in a network containing many interacting units. Model behaviour must instead be evaluated through appropriate validation, sensitivity analysis, and explanation methods when needed.

Study instruction:
- "Work through a numerical example using two inputs, two weights, a bias, and an activation function."

Key point:
- Weights control the contribution of inputs, while biases provide adjustable offsets.
- Learned parameter values alone do not establish causation or guarantee an interpretable explanation.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- Google for Developers — Neural Network Activation Functions
6. Q: What is a hidden layer, and why does the number of layers matter?
A: Show answer
A hidden layer is an intermediate stage of a neural network that transforms information between the input and output. Different layers can learn representations at different levels of abstraction. In image recognition, for example, intermediate representations may capture simple visual structures before later representations combine them into more complex patterns. A network with multiple hidden layers is often described as deep, although the precise definition can vary by context. Additional layers can increase a model's expressive capacity, but they also add parameters or computational operations depending on the architecture and may make training more difficult. The best depth is not determined by a universal rule. It should be selected through validation, informed by the problem, the quantity and structure of available data, computational limits, and the risk of overfitting. More layers do not guarantee better generalization.

Study instruction:
- "Compare shallow and deeper network designs and explain how validation results influence architecture selection."

Key point:
- Hidden layers create intermediate representations that support complex predictions.
- Network depth should be justified by measured performance rather than assumed superiority.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- Keras — Computer Vision Examples
7. Q: What is a feed-forward neural network, and how does information move through it?
A: Show answer
In a feed-forward neural network, information moves from the input through intermediate layers toward the output without a cyclic connection that sends the current result back into an earlier layer as part of the same forward computation. Each layer applies learned transformations, and the final output represents the model's prediction. Feed-forward networks are used for tasks such as classification and regression. Training can involve repeatedly running the forward computation, comparing predictions with target values through a loss function, calculating gradients, and updating parameters. The distinction between forward computation and training is important: a network may perform inference without changing its learned parameters, whereas training explicitly updates them. A network's output can also become input to a subsequent model in a larger pipeline without making the original network recurrent.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one example from input to prediction and identify where the forward pass ends and the training update begins."

Key point:
- Feed-forward networks process information through a directed sequence of layers.
- Forward prediction and parameter training are related but distinct operations.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- Google for Developers — Neural Network Training Using Backpropagation
8. Q: What is an activation function, and why is nonlinearity necessary in deep learning?
A: Show answer
An activation function transforms a neuron's pre-activation value before the result is passed to the next stage. Common functions include sigmoid, hyperbolic tangent, and the rectified linear unit, or ReLU. Nonlinear activations allow a neural network to represent nonlinear relationships between inputs and outputs. Without suitable nonlinear operations, stacking ordinary linear transformations would still produce a linear transformation, limiting the relationships the network could represent. The activation function also affects the flow of gradients during training and can influence optimization stability. No activation function is universally best: choices depend on the architecture, layer, output requirements, optimization behaviour, and problem. Output layers may use functions suited to the task, while hidden layers often use activations selected for effective training and representation learning.

Study instruction:
- "Explain why multiple linear layers without nonlinear activations cannot represent arbitrary nonlinear relationships."

Key point:
- Nonlinear activation functions expand the relationships a neural network can learn.
- Activation choices should reflect the architecture and prediction task.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Network Activation Functions
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
9. Q: How do sigmoid, tanh, and ReLU activation functions differ?
A: Show answer
The sigmoid function maps real-valued inputs into a range between zero and one, making it useful in certain probability-related output settings. The hyperbolic tangent, or tanh, maps values between negative one and positive one. ReLU returns zero for negative inputs and returns the input itself for non-negative inputs. Sigmoid and tanh can experience very small gradients when their inputs lie in saturated regions, slowing the training of affected layers. ReLU is computationally simple and can reduce some vanishing-gradient problems in suitable networks, but units can become inactive if their outputs remain zero. Other activation functions and variants may address particular training requirements. An activation that works well in a hidden layer may not be suitable for an output layer. For example, a binary classification output may use sigmoid, while a multiclass output may use a normalized probability-producing function.

Study instruction:
- "Calculate the outputs of sigmoid, tanh, and ReLU for positive, negative, and zero inputs, then identify suitable use cases."

Key point:
- Sigmoid, tanh, and ReLU have different output ranges and optimization characteristics.
- Choosing an activation requires attention to both the layer's role and its training behaviour.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Network Activation Functions
- Google for Developers — Neural Network Training Using Backpropagation
10. Q: What is a multilayer perceptron, and how is it used?
A: Show answer
A multilayer perceptron, or MLP, is a neural network consisting of multiple layers of interconnected units, generally with feed-forward connections between layers. Its hidden layers transform input variables into increasingly useful representations, and its output layer generates a prediction. An MLP can be used for classification, regression, and other structured prediction tasks. It is often a useful baseline for tabular or numerical problems, although it may not exploit spatial or temporal structure as naturally as specialized architectures. MLP performance depends on feature preparation, architecture, regularization, optimization, and data quality. Adding hidden layers or units increases capacity but can also increase computation and overfitting risk. A fair evaluation compares the MLP with alternative models using the same data splits and an appropriate metric.

Study instruction:
- "Design a small MLP for a classification task and identify its inputs, hidden layers, output layer, and evaluation metric."

Key point:
- An MLP learns layered transformations for structured prediction tasks.
- Specialized architectures may be more suitable when inputs contain strong spatial or sequential structure.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- Keras — Computer Vision Examples
11. Q: How does a neural network turn a prediction error into a training objective?
A: Show answer
A neural network normally uses a loss function to quantify disagreement between a model's prediction and the relevant target or objective. The loss function depends on the task. Classification may use cross-entropy, while regression may use measures such as mean squared error or mean absolute error. During training, the model calculates predictions, evaluates the loss, and uses optimization procedures to change its parameters. The chosen loss function influences what the model is encouraged to learn, so it must be consistent with the problem and the consequences of different errors. Minimizing a numerical loss does not automatically mean maximizing real-world usefulness. A model can optimize the wrong objective, exploit data leakage, or perform poorly for important groups even when its training loss is low. Validation metrics and operational requirements must therefore complement the training objective.

Study instruction:
- "Select a loss function for a classification or regression task and explain what kinds of prediction errors it penalizes."

Key point:
- The loss function defines the objective used to guide model optimization.
- A low training loss is not sufficient evidence of practical or generalizable performance.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
12. Q: What is backpropagation, and why is it central to neural network training?
A: Show answer
Backpropagation computes gradients of a training objective with respect to a network's parameters by applying the chain rule through the network's computational operations. In practical terms, it determines how changes in weights and biases would affect the loss locally. An optimizer then uses those gradients to update parameters. Backpropagation is not itself the same as every optimization algorithm, and it does not guarantee that a model will find the globally best possible parameter values. Its importance lies in making gradient-based training of multilayer networks computationally practical. Training involves repeating forward computation, loss calculation, gradient calculation, and parameter updates across batches and epochs. When gradients are unstable, gradients are incorrect, or the model is poorly configured, training may fail to converge or may produce a model that does not generalize well.

Study instruction:
- "Draw the training loop and distinguish the forward pass, loss calculation, backpropagation, and optimizer update."

Key point:
- Backpropagation calculates gradients that reveal how parameters influence the loss.
- The optimizer uses those gradients to update the model, but successful training is not guaranteed.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Network Training Using Backpropagation
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
13. Q: How do gradient descent and learning rates affect model training?
A: Show answer
Gradient descent and related optimization algorithms update model parameters in directions intended to reduce the loss. The learning rate controls the scale of parameter updates. If it is too high, training may oscillate, diverge, or fail to settle into a useful region. If it is too low, training may progress very slowly or appear to stall. Other issues include vanishing gradients, exploding gradients, poor initialization, unsuitable batch sizes, and noisy data. Training diagnostics often include loss curves, validation performance, gradient behaviour, and evidence that parameters are updating correctly. Techniques such as adaptive optimizers, learning-rate schedules, gradient clipping, normalization, or alternative activations may help, depending on the failure mode. These are tools for investigation rather than automatic solutions; changing several parameters at once can make the cause of an improvement or failure difficult to identify.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect hypothetical training and validation curves and decide whether to investigate the learning rate, gradient stability, model capacity, or data quality."

Key point:
- The learning rate influences the stability and speed of optimization.
- Training problems should be diagnosed using evidence before configuration changes are made.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Network Training Using Backpropagation
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
14. Q: Why must training, validation, and test datasets be kept separate?
A: Show answer
The training dataset is used to fit model parameters. The validation dataset supports decisions such as architecture selection, hyperparameter tuning, threshold setting, and early stopping. The test dataset is reserved for a final evaluation after development choices have been made. If the same test observations repeatedly influence model development, the reported test performance can become optimistic because the process has indirectly adapted to them. Dataset separation must also prevent leakage caused by duplicate records, future information, repeated observations from the same person, or related images crossing between splits. For temporal tasks, splits should respect time ordering where appropriate. For grouped data, observations from one individual or source may need to remain together. The correct design depends on how the model will be used and what kind of generalization must be demonstrated.

Study instruction:
- "Create a dataset-splitting plan and identify possible leakage through duplicate images, repeated subjects, or future information."

Key point:
- Training, validation, and test datasets have different purposes.
- Data leakage can make evaluation results look substantially better than real-world performance.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
15. Q: How does data quality influence deep learning performance?
A: Show answer
Deep learning models depend on the information represented in their training data. Missing values, inconsistent labels, duplicated records, measurement errors, unrepresentative examples, sampling bias, and corrupted inputs can all affect learning and evaluation. Large datasets are not necessarily good datasets: a large collection can still be systematically biased, poorly labelled, or irrelevant to the deployment environment. Data quality should be assessed through provenance, coverage, label consistency, duplicate detection, class distribution, measurement procedures, and comparison with the intended use population. It is also necessary to determine whether sensitive or restricted information is being used appropriately. Data cleaning should not silently remove difficult but valid cases, because this can hide an important source of operational failure. Documenting data preparation decisions makes the results easier to reproduce and challenge.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a data-quality checklist covering provenance, completeness, labelling, representativeness, duplication, and privacy."

Key point:
- Data quality and representativeness directly affect model reliability.
- Increasing dataset size does not automatically eliminate bias or error.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
16. Q: Why is data labelling a major part of supervised deep learning?
A: Show answer
In supervised learning, a model uses training examples paired with labels or target values. For example, an image dataset may label each picture with an object category. The model learns statistical relationships between the input and those target values. If labels are inaccurate, inconsistent, ambiguous, or based on the wrong definition, the model may learn the wrong relationships even when its optimization process works correctly. Labelling requires clear instructions, suitable expertise, quality checks, and a process for resolving disagreements. Crowdsourcing can increase the volume of labelled examples, but it still requires validation and consistent annotation standards. Some tasks also require careful handling of uncertain or subjective labels. Label quality should be measured and monitored, especially when predictions affect people or high-stakes decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Design annotation guidance for an image classification dataset and describe how uncertain labels and disagreements should be reviewed."

Key point:
- Supervised models learn from the target information provided in their training data.
- Clear annotation standards and quality controls reduce label-related errors.

Source links:
- ImageNet — Image Database and Research Resource
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
17. Q: What is overfitting, and how can it be detected and reduced?
A: Show answer
Overfitting occurs when a model learns patterns that fit its training data but fail to generalize adequately to new observations. A common warning sign is that training performance continues to improve while validation performance stops improving or deteriorates. Causes may include excessive model capacity, limited or unrepresentative data, data leakage, and an overly long training process. Mitigation methods include early stopping based on validation results, regularization, dropout, data augmentation where appropriate, simpler architectures, and collecting more representative data. Each method has limitations. For example, augmentation is useful only when the transformations preserve the correct meaning of the input. A final test set should remain separate from repeated development decisions. Evaluation on an independent external dataset can provide additional evidence that the model generalizes beyond its original environment.

Study instruction:
- "Interpret training and validation curves and recommend a specific overfitting control based on the observed pattern."

Key point:
- Training performance alone does not demonstrate generalization.
- Overfitting controls should be selected and evaluated according to the specific cause and task.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
- Google for Developers — Neural Network Training Using Backpropagation
18. Q: What is a recurrent neural network, and why is it useful for sequential data?
A: Show answer
A recurrent neural network, or RNN, is an architecture designed to process sequences in which earlier information can influence later computations through recurrent state. This makes it relevant to temporal and ordered data, including language sequences, sensor measurements, and other time-dependent observations. Unlike a basic feed-forward network, an RNN maintains information across sequence steps. Training can use backpropagation through time, which propagates gradients across the sequence. Long sequences can make learning difficult because gradients may vanish or explode, and the model may struggle to retain useful information over long intervals. An RNN's performance also depends on how inputs are sequenced, how missing observations are handled, and whether the training data reflects future operational conditions. Modern sequence tasks may also use architectures such as Transformers, so RNNs should be compared with suitable alternatives rather than treated as the universal solution for ordered data.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a feed-forward network and an RNN using a sequence prediction example, and explain how previous inputs influence later outputs."

Key point:
- RNNs are designed to model dependencies across ordered sequences.
- Sequence length, gradient stability, and alternative architectures influence model choice.

Source links:
- MIT Press — Long Short-Term Memory
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
19. Q: What is long short-term memory, and how does it address limitations of ordinary RNNs?
A: Show answer
Long short-term memory, or LSTM, is a recurrent architecture designed to help sequence models retain and manage information across longer time intervals. It uses internal state and gating mechanisms to regulate what information is retained, added, or exposed. This design was developed to address difficulties associated with learning long-range dependencies using conventional recurrent training methods, including vanishing gradients. LSTMs have been applied to language processing, time-series prediction, and other sequential problems. They do not guarantee that every long-term dependency will be learned, and they can still require substantial data, careful tuning, and appropriate validation. For many current sequence tasks, alternatives such as attention-based models may perform better. The appropriate architecture should be chosen through comparative experiments under a suitable evaluation design.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the purpose of LSTM gating and compare an LSTM with a basic RNN for a long-sequence prediction task."

Key point:
- LSTMs use gated state to improve the handling of longer-term sequence information.
- Their suitability must be established through task-specific evaluation and comparison.

Source links:
- MIT Press — Long Short-Term Memory
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
20. Q: What is a convolutional neural network, and why is it commonly used for image analysis?
A: Show answer
A convolutional neural network, or CNN, is an architecture that uses convolutional operations to process local patterns in data such as images. Convolutional filters can detect useful spatial structures while sharing learned parameters across image locations. As representations pass through the network, the model can combine local information into increasingly complex visual features. Other components, such as nonlinear activations and pooling or downsampling operations, may help build suitable representations and manage computational cost. CNNs have been used for image classification, object detection, medical image analysis, and other vision tasks. However, a CNN can still be sensitive to distribution changes, irrelevant correlations, poor labels, and visual features that do not reliably indicate the target. Model performance should therefore be tested on representative inputs, including difficult and unfamiliar cases.

Study instruction:
- "Trace how an image passes through convolutional layers and explain how local features can contribute to a final classification."

Key point:
- CNNs use spatially structured operations to learn visual representations.
- Good image classification results do not eliminate the need for external validation and error analysis.

Source links:
- Proceedings of the IEEE — Gradient-Based Learning Applied to Document Recognition
- Keras — Computer Vision Examples
21. Q: How do generative adversarial networks work?
A: Show answer
A generative adversarial network, or GAN, uses two neural networks trained in competition. The generator attempts to produce synthetic examples that resemble data from a target distribution, while the discriminator attempts to distinguish real training examples from generated ones. The generator is trained to produce outputs that the discriminator finds increasingly convincing. This adversarial process can support image synthesis, image transformation, and other generative tasks. Training can be unstable because the two networks influence one another, and progress depends on how well their objectives and capacity remain balanced. A GAN may produce visually convincing outputs that are still inaccurate, biased, or inconsistent with the intended use. Generated data should therefore be evaluated for quality, diversity, fidelity, and potential harm, rather than judged only by how realistic a few examples appear.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the separate objectives of a GAN generator and discriminator and identify two risks of relying on visual realism alone."

Key point:
- GANs learn to generate data through competition between a generator and a discriminator.
- Realistic-looking synthetic output is not necessarily accurate, diverse, or harmless.

Source links:
- arXiv — Generative Adversarial Networks
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
22. Q: What stages should a practical deep learning project follow from problem definition to evaluation?
A: Show answer
A practical project should begin by defining the decision or task, intended users, constraints, and consequences of mistakes. The team then identifies available data, verifies its suitability and permissions, defines labels or target values, and establishes a baseline model. It creates appropriate training, validation, and test datasets, selects an architecture and objective, and conducts controlled experiments. Performance is evaluated using metrics that match the task, with attention to error types, group differences, and generalization. Before deployment, the team verifies technical reliability, security, workflow integration, monitoring, human oversight, and procedures for responding to failures. After deployment, performance must be observed because incoming data and operational conditions can change. A trained model is only one component of a complete system, which also needs documentation, version control, ownership, and maintenance arrangements.

Study instruction:
- "Create a project lifecycle checklist covering problem definition, data, baseline, training, validation, deployment, monitoring, and retirement."

Key point:
- Deep learning is an end-to-end engineering and governance process, not merely model training.
- Deployment requires monitoring and operational controls after evaluation is complete.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
23. Q: How should a team evaluate a deep learning model's accuracy and practical usefulness?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should begin with a clear definition of the target and the conditions under which the model will operate. For classification, a team may examine precision, recall, specificity, F1 score, confusion matrices, and other appropriate metrics. For regression or forecasting, it may use measures such as mean absolute error or root mean squared error. No single metric is appropriate for every task. Class imbalance can make overall accuracy misleading, while an apparently strong average score can hide poor performance for an important subgroup. A model should also be compared with a reasonable baseline and, where possible, evaluated on data collected independently of model development. Thresholds should reflect the relative consequences of false positives and false negatives. Final evaluation should cover practical factors such as latency, reliability, cost, accessibility, and the time needed for a human to review incorrect or uncertain predictions.

Study instruction:
- "Choose appropriate metrics for one classification task and one forecasting task, and explain the limitations of each selected metric."

Key point:
- Model evaluation must reflect the task, data distribution, and consequences of errors.
- Average accuracy can hide important weaknesses in specific groups or scenarios.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
24. Q: How can deep learning be applied to image recognition and classification?
A: Show answer
Image classification assigns one or more labels to an image based on patterns learned from examples. A trained model may distinguish animals, identify objects, categorize manufacturing defects, or recognize visual patterns in scientific data. A successful system requires representative images, consistent labels, suitable preprocessing, appropriate model architecture, and testing on images not used to fit the model. Evaluation should examine confusing classes, unusual lighting, changes in camera or image quality, and backgrounds that may accidentally correlate with labels. For example, a classifier might appear to recognize an animal while actually relying on scenery that frequently appears in the training set. Object detection and segmentation involve different outputs from simple image classification, so performance requirements must match the task. The model should be assessed on the intended real-world workflow rather than on a benchmark alone.

Study instruction:
- "Design an image classification evaluation plan that includes held-out images, confusing categories, unusual conditions, and an analysis of incorrect predictions."

Key point:
- Image classification learns visual patterns from labelled examples.
- Testing should establish whether the model identifies the relevant objects rather than accidental background cues.

Source links:
- ImageNet — Image Database and Research Resource
- Keras — Computer Vision Examples
25. Q: What can deep learning contribute to medical imaging and the early identification of disease?
A: Show answer
Deep learning can analyze complex imaging patterns that may be difficult to detect consistently through manual review alone. Research has investigated models that use PET scans or other medical images to identify patterns associated with conditions such as Alzheimer's disease. Such studies illustrate the potential value of automated pattern recognition, but a research result is not the same as an approved clinical tool. Medical applications require appropriate patient data, clinically relevant evaluation, representative populations, external validation, and assessment of false-positive and false-negative consequences. Imaging systems can be affected by differences in scanners, hospitals, acquisition protocols, demographics, and prevalence. A model's prediction should be interpreted within the clinical process and not treated as independent proof of a diagnosis. Clinical professionals and applicable regulatory requirements remain central to the design and use of high-stakes medical systems.

Study instruction:
- "Review a medical imaging study and identify its training population, independent evaluation data, reported performance, limitations, and proposed clinical role."

Key point:
- Deep learning may identify complex patterns in medical images and support clinical assessment.
- Clinical usefulness requires validation, oversight, and evidence beyond experimental accuracy.

- FDA — Good Machine Learning Practice for Medical Device Development
26. Q: What role can deep learning play in radiology and medical image enhancement?
A: Show answer
Deep learning can support radiology through image reconstruction, image enhancement, segmentation, detection, classification, and workflow prioritization. Some systems aim to produce usable images from fewer measurements or shorter acquisition processes, while others highlight suspicious findings for further review. These uses involve distinct clinical and technical objectives. An image-enhancement model must not create misleading features, suppress relevant abnormalities, or make an image look convincing without preserving clinically meaningful information. Evaluation should examine diagnostic performance, failure cases, image fidelity, compatibility with scanners and protocols, and how clinicians interact with the output. Human oversight should be defined according to the clinical risk and the approved purpose of the system. Claims about improved speed or quality should be supported by documented testing rather than visual impressions alone.

Study instruction:
- "Distinguish image enhancement from diagnostic classification and list the evidence required before an enhanced image can be used in a clinical workflow."

Key point:
- Radiology applications include both image processing and decision-support tasks.
- Visual quality alone does not prove that medically important information has been preserved.

Source links:
- FDA — Good Machine Learning Practice for Medical Device Development
- FDA — AI-Enabled Device Software Functions: Lifecycle Management and Marketing Submission Recommendations
27. Q: How can deep learning improve wind energy forecasting and operational planning?
A: Show answer
Wind power output varies with weather and operating conditions, which makes it difficult to plan electricity generation precisely. A forecasting model can learn relationships between weather forecasts, historical turbine measurements, and later power output. Operators can use these predictions to support scheduling, delivery commitments, and resource planning. Forecasting does not remove the underlying variability of wind, and its value depends on how well predictions support operational decisions. Evaluation should compare forecasts with actual output over suitable periods and consider both predictive error and the costs associated with different kinds of forecast mistakes. Weather forecast quality, changes in turbine behaviour, and shifts in operating conditions can all affect results. The model therefore needs reliable source data, operational monitoring, and a clear process for handling uncertainty and forecast failures.

Study instruction:
- "Map weather inputs, predicted wind generation, forecast errors, and the operational decisions that depend on the forecast."

Key point:
- Deep learning can help predict variable renewable energy output from historical and forecast data.
- Operational value depends on forecast quality and the consequences of decisions based on predictions.

- Google DeepMind — Machine Learning Can Boost the Value of Wind Energy
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
28. Q: How can deep learning help earthquake monitoring and early-warning research?
A: Show answer
Earthquake monitoring systems collect large volumes of seismic measurements that can contain complex signals, background noise, overlapping events, and patterns that are difficult to identify consistently using simple rules. Machine learning models can help detect candidate earthquake signals, classify waveforms, estimate event characteristics, and support the processing of seismic datasets. In an early-warning system, processing speed matters because alerts depend on detecting and interpreting signals before damaging waves arrive at particular locations. However, false alarms and missed events can have serious consequences. Evaluation should therefore include detection delay, false-positive rates, missed-event rates, robustness to noise, and performance on events that were not used during training. Deep learning should support scientific validation and operational safeguards rather than replace them. Researchers must also consider whether models generalize across locations and seismic monitoring conditions.

Study instruction:
- "Describe how a seismic monitoring model would be tested for detection accuracy, speed, noise sensitivity, and false-warning risk."

Key point:
- Deep learning can help process complex seismic data and identify potential earthquake signals.
- High-stakes early-warning applications require rigorous testing of delays and both types of error.

- Caltech — Creating a Virtual Seismologist
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
29. Q: How can deep learning contribute to cybersecurity and the detection of evolving threats?
A: Show answer
Cybersecurity systems can use deep learning to analyze network activity, files, sequences of events, or other data for patterns associated with malicious behaviour. This can be valuable when threats vary in appearance or cannot be described adequately by a fixed set of manually written signatures. Nevertheless, a model trained on historical threats may fail to recognize new attack techniques, and malicious actors may deliberately modify their behaviour to evade detection. Security teams must evaluate false alarms, missed attacks, performance on previously unseen threats, and the consequences of blocking legitimate activity. Models should operate alongside other security controls, threat intelligence, incident response processes, and human investigation. Training data must also be protected against poisoning and unauthorized manipulation. A high detection score on a historical dataset is not sufficient evidence that a system will remain effective against evolving adversaries.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a cybersecurity evaluation plan that includes novel threats, false alarms, missed attacks, data manipulation, and analyst review."

Key point:
- Deep learning can detect complex patterns that complement conventional threat-detection methods.
- Adversarial adaptation and changing attack patterns require ongoing testing and monitoring.

- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
30. Q: What deep learning applications are suited to time-series forecasting and sequential prediction?
A: Show answer
Time-series forecasting uses observations recorded over time to estimate future values or events. Examples include energy generation, equipment condition, demand, environmental measurements, and certain financial or operational indicators. RNNs and LSTMs are examples of architectures designed to process sequential relationships, while other neural architectures can also be used for forecasting. A sound forecasting design must prevent future information from entering the training data and must use a validation approach that reflects the intended forecast horizon. It should compare predictions with simple baselines and consider changing seasonal patterns, missing readings, sensor failures, and unusual events. Forecast accuracy should be measured at the relevant operational horizon. A model that performs well one step ahead may be unsuitable for a much longer planning horizon.

Study instruction:
- "Design a time-ordered training and testing strategy for a forecasting task and compare the model with a simple historical baseline."

Key point:
- Sequential models can learn temporal patterns in time-series data.
- Time-aware validation and baseline comparisons are essential for credible forecasting results.

- MIT Press — Long Short-Term Memory

31. Q: Why are GPUs and specialized accelerators important to deep learning?
A: Show answer
Training deep neural networks involves repeated numerical operations over large arrays of data and parameters. Graphics processing units, or GPUs, can perform many of these operations in parallel and are therefore widely used in deep learning. Other accelerators, including tensor-focused processors, can improve performance for suitable workloads. Hardware selection depends on model size, memory requirements, numerical precision, software support, available infrastructure, throughput, and cost. A more powerful accelerator does not automatically improve a model's quality: it may only make training or inference faster. Workloads also need to be measured because data loading, preprocessing, communication, or inefficient code can become bottlenecks. Organizations should compare total operating costs, including hardware access, storage, electricity, maintenance, and specialist engineering time, rather than comparing advertised computing performance alone.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the main computational bottlenecks in a proposed deep learning workload and determine which hardware requirements must be benchmarked."

Key point:
- Accelerators can reduce the time required for suitable neural network computations.
- Hardware decisions must consider memory, software compatibility, total cost, and actual workload performance.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- Keras — Computer Vision Examples
32. Q: How does model training differ from inference?
A: Show answer
Training is the process of fitting model parameters using data and an optimization objective. It generally involves forward computation, loss calculation, gradient calculation, and parameter updates. Inference uses an already trained model to produce outputs for new inputs without ordinarily updating the model's learned parameters. Training often requires repeated processing of data and can demand substantial computing resources, while inference costs depend on the model, input size, hardware, and required latency or throughput. Some applications require large numbers of low-latency predictions, while others process data in batches. Deployment planning should measure both training and inference requirements, because a model that is affordable to train may be too expensive or too slow to operate. Production systems must also control which trained model version is used and ensure that only authorized changes reach deployment.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the computational steps and operational requirements of training a model and using it to make predictions in production."

Key point:
- Training learns model parameters, while inference applies the trained model to new inputs.
- Training cost and production inference cost should be assessed separately.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Neural Networks
- Keras — Computer Vision Examples
33. Q: When should a team choose deep learning instead of a simpler machine learning approach?
A: Show answer
Deep learning is worth considering when the problem involves complex patterns, rich unstructured data, or representations that are difficult to engineer manually, and when suitable training data and resources are available. A simpler model may be preferable when the dataset is small, the task has relatively straightforward relationships, results must be easily explained, or inference resources are constrained. The comparison should include predictive performance, calibration, robustness, interpretability, training and operating cost, development time, and maintenance. Teams should begin with a reasonable baseline and establish what additional performance would justify a more complex system. A complex architecture that offers little practical improvement can create unnecessary costs and operational risk. Conversely, a demanding computer vision or audio problem may justify deep learning because simpler approaches cannot represent the required patterns effectively. Evidence from controlled experiments should determine the choice.

Study instruction:
- "Create a model-selection comparison that includes predictive quality, interpretability, data volume, computation, maintenance, and deployment risk."

Key point:
- Deep learning is appropriate when its strengths match the task and its constraints.
- A simpler model is preferable when it delivers the required results with less complexity or risk.

- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
34. Q: Why can a deep learning model perform well without being explainable to users?
A: Show answer
A deep neural network may contain many layers, parameters, and interactions that are difficult to describe in ordinary human terms. High predictive performance measures how well the model performs according to particular evaluation criteria; it does not establish that people can understand why a particular output was produced. Explanation methods may examine input sensitivity, highlight influential features, summarize model behaviour, or provide examples relevant to a prediction. However, an explanation can itself be incomplete, unstable, or misleading if it does not faithfully represent the model's behaviour. The appropriate level of explainability depends on the intended use, affected people, regulatory obligations, and consequences of incorrect results. In higher-risk applications, teams may need stronger documentation, interpretability analysis, explicit limitations, and a process for challenging or reviewing individual decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Distinguish predictive accuracy from explainability and identify the explanation evidence appropriate for a high-stakes application."

Key point:
- Accurate predictions do not automatically produce understandable explanations.
- Explanation methods must be assessed for fidelity and suitability to their audience and use.

- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
35. Q: Why should model correlations not be interpreted as proof of causation?
A: Show answer
Deep learning models typically learn statistical patterns that help predict target values. A strong association between an input and an output does not, by itself, demonstrate that the input causes the outcome. A model may rely on background scenery, hospital-specific scanning patterns, demographic proxies, or other features that correlate with a label in the training data but are not the intended basis for the prediction. Such shortcuts can make the model appear successful while reducing reliability when the environment changes. Investigating causality may require study designs, domain expertise, experiments, or other analytical methods beyond ordinary predictive evaluation. Teams should test whether model performance depends on plausible task-relevant information and whether known confounders explain apparent relationships. This is especially important when predictions are used to justify interventions or decisions that assume a causal relationship.

Study instruction:
- "Construct an example in which a model learns a misleading correlation and explain how a change in the data environment could expose the problem."

Key point:
- Predictive relationships are not automatically causal relationships.
- Domain analysis and targeted testing help detect reliance on misleading correlations.

Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
36. Q: What are the common limitations of deep learning in unfamiliar or open-ended situations?
A: Show answer
Deep learning models can perform very well on the patterns represented in their training data while remaining unreliable when confronted with unfamiliar conditions, ambiguous instructions, unusual combinations of features, or changes in the task itself. Models may lack the robust common-sense reasoning that people expect from systems that appear capable in narrow settings. A network trained for one prediction task should not be assumed to transfer its competence automatically to unrelated tasks. Some limitations can be reduced through better data coverage, carefully designed objectives, external validation, additional system components, and human oversight. Others require changing the model architecture or the overall approach. Testing should include edge cases, out-of-distribution inputs, ambiguous situations, and cases in which the model should abstain or refer the decision to a human reviewer.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare challenging test cases that fall outside common training examples and specify what the system should do when it cannot provide a reliable prediction."

Key point:
- Strong performance on familiar examples does not guarantee dependable performance in unfamiliar situations.
- Safe systems need explicit handling of ambiguity, unsupported inputs, and uncertain predictions.

Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
37. Q: How can bias and unrepresentative data affect deep learning outcomes?
A: Show answer
Bias can arise when training data overrepresents certain populations, locations, devices, conditions, or behaviours while underrepresenting others. A model may achieve strong average results while producing substantially worse predictions for a particular group or operating context. Bias can also arise from labels, historical decisions, target definitions, measurement processes, and the way model outputs are used. Evaluating fairness requires identifying the populations affected, choosing meaningful subgroup metrics, examining error differences, and understanding whether the target itself is appropriate. There is no single fairness measure that resolves every application, and different requirements can conflict. Teams should document the evaluation criteria and limitations, consult relevant stakeholders, and establish processes for addressing harmful discrepancies. Bias checks should continue after deployment because both data and usage patterns may change.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a subgroup evaluation plan and explain how you would investigate a significant difference in error rates between populations."

Key point:
- Unrepresentative data can create uneven performance across groups and environments.
- Fairness assessment requires defined populations, suitable metrics, and documented follow-up actions.

Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
38. Q: What privacy and data-governance issues arise when training deep learning systems?
A: Show answer
Deep learning projects may use personal information, medical images, audio recordings, behavioural data, or other sensitive records. Before data collection or use, organizations should establish the lawful basis, permissions, purpose, access restrictions, retention arrangements, and relevant consent or contractual requirements. Removing obvious identifiers does not always eliminate the risk that individuals could be reidentified or that a model could reveal sensitive information. Teams should minimize data collection, control access, protect data in transit and at rest, record data provenance, and use appropriate security safeguards. They should also assess whether trained models or generated outputs may disclose information from training examples. Governance requirements depend on the jurisdiction, application, dataset, and contractual context, and must be checked rather than assumed. Privacy safeguards should remain part of the project lifecycle rather than being added only before release.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a privacy review covering data provenance, purpose limitation, access control, retention, reidentification risk, and model outputs."

Key point:
- Sensitive training data creates obligations throughout collection, training, deployment, and retention.
- Removing direct identifiers does not eliminate every privacy risk.

Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- FDA — Good Machine Learning Practice for Medical Device Development
39. Q: How can deepfakes and synthetic media create risks when generative models become more capable?
A: Show answer
Generative models can create synthetic images, audio, video, or other content that resembles authentic material. Such outputs can support legitimate creative work, simulation, accessibility, and research, but they can also be used for impersonation, misinformation, fraud, or unauthorized manipulation of someone's likeness or voice. The risk is not limited to whether a generated example looks convincing. A synthetic output may be distributed without context, combined with other information, or used to influence decisions. Controls can include provenance records, disclosure practices, authentication mechanisms, content verification, access restrictions, and incident-response procedures. Detection systems can help but should not be assumed to identify every synthetic item reliably. Organizations must evaluate the specific threat model and relevant legal, ethical, and consent requirements before generating or publishing synthetic media.

Study instruction:
- "Describe one legitimate and one harmful use of synthetic media, and identify preventive and detective controls for each scenario."

Key point:
- Generative models can create useful synthetic data and realistic media that can also be misused.
- Provenance, consent, verification, and responsible distribution help manage synthetic-media risks.

Source links:
- arXiv — Generative Adversarial Networks
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
40. Q: How do adversarial inputs and small changes affect deep learning reliability?
A: Show answer
Some models can be misled by inputs deliberately constructed to trigger incorrect outputs. Other models may be sensitive to seemingly minor changes in lighting, image compression, background context, sensor readings, or data preprocessing. Such failures can arise because the learned decision boundary does not correspond perfectly to the concepts people expect the model to recognize. Testing should include realistic variations, corrupted inputs, unusual combinations, and threat-specific adversarial examples where relevant. Mitigations may involve more diverse training data, augmentation, robust training objectives, input checks, detection mechanisms, and fallback procedures. No single defence guarantees immunity from every attack or distribution shift. Security testing should reflect the application's threat model, while operational safeguards should define when a prediction is rejected, reviewed, or prevented from triggering an irreversible action.

Study instruction:
- "Create a robustness test plan for plausible input variations and describe what the system should do when a prediction becomes unreliable."

Key point:
- High benchmark accuracy does not guarantee robustness to manipulated or unusual inputs.
- Robustness measures must be tested against realistic conditions and defined threat scenarios.

Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
41. Q: What causes deep learning models to degrade after deployment?
A: Show answer
Model performance can decline when the environment changes relative to the training data. Data drift occurs when the distribution or characteristics of incoming inputs change. Changes in the relationship between inputs and outcomes can also undermine model predictions. Examples include new camera equipment, evolving cybersecurity threats, modified clinical practices, shifts in customer behaviour, or changed environmental patterns. Monitoring should track input quality, missing fields, feature distributions, prediction patterns, operational outcomes, error rates when labels become available, and relevant subgroup performance. A drift alert does not automatically prove the model is failing; investigation is needed to determine the source and significance. Responses may include data correction, threshold adjustment, a controlled fallback, further evaluation, or retraining. Any material model change should undergo appropriate validation before replacing the deployed version.

Study instruction:
- "Design a monitoring dashboard that distinguishes data-quality alerts, input drift, predictive performance deterioration, and confirmed operational harm."

Key point:
- Deployment environments can change and invalidate assumptions made during training.
- Monitoring must connect detected changes to investigation, decisions, and documented corrective action.

Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
42. Q: Why are external validation and independent testing important for high-stakes models?
A: Show answer
A model can appear successful when evaluated on held-out data drawn from the same source used during development but perform poorly in a different hospital, region, organization, device, or operational setting. External validation tests whether the model remains useful under conditions distinct from those represented in the original development data. Independent testing is especially important when incorrect predictions can affect health, safety, security, or major financial decisions. The evaluation should reflect relevant populations, data collection methods, workflow conditions, and error consequences. When the model is recalibrated or adapted to a new environment, the changes should be documented and evaluated appropriately. External validation does not guarantee permanent reliability, but it provides stronger evidence about generalization than repeated testing on the original dataset alone.

Study instruction:
- "Propose an external validation plan for a model moving from one institution or region to another and identify the conditions that could limit transferability."

Key point:
- Performance on one dataset may not transfer to a different operational environment.
- Independent validation provides additional evidence before wider or higher-stakes use.

Source links:
- FDA — Good Machine Learning Practice for Medical Device Development
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
43. Q: How should data leakage be investigated when a model has unexpectedly high accuracy?
A: Show answer
Unexpectedly strong results can indicate a genuinely effective model, but they can also result from errors in dataset construction, target leakage, duplicate examples, or a metric that does not match the intended task. Target leakage occurs when information unavailable at prediction time influences training or evaluation. Duplicate or near-duplicate images across training and test sets can make evaluation artificially easy. For temporal data, future observations may inadvertently enter the training features. Investigators should review the data pipeline, timestamps, identifiers, target construction, transformations, dataset splits, and evaluation code. They should compare against a simple baseline and rerun tests with a carefully constructed independent split. If leakage is found, performance should be recalculated using a valid evaluation design, and any previous claims based on contaminated results should be corrected.

Study instruction:
- "Audit a high-performing model for target leakage, duplicate observations, inappropriate split logic, and features unavailable at decision time."

Key point:
- Unexpected accuracy should be investigated rather than automatically accepted.
- Data and evaluation errors can create performance results that do not reflect real-world capability.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
44. Q: How should deep learning experiments be documented and made reproducible?
A: Show answer
Reproducibility requires a record of the data version, data preparation steps, architecture, parameter settings, initialization, optimization method, software dependencies, computing environment, training procedure, evaluation splits, random seeds where applicable, and final metrics. Exact reproduction may still be affected by nondeterministic hardware operations, software changes, or unavailable external data. Nevertheless, controlled versioning and experiment logs make it easier to investigate unexpected results and compare alternative models fairly. Teams should also preserve the selected model artifact, configuration, evaluation report, documented limitations, and authorization for deployment. Changes to training data, preprocessing, thresholds, architecture, or software libraries should be recorded and evaluated. A performance result without enough information to identify the model and reproduce its evaluation is difficult to verify and unsafe to treat as the permanent reference result.

Study instruction:
- "Create an experiment record template with data version, model configuration, training settings, evaluation results, and deployment approval."

Key point:
- Reproducibility depends on traceable data, code, configuration, and evaluation evidence.
- Material changes should produce a new identifiable version with documented testing.

Source links:
- Google for Developers — Machine Learning Crash Course
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
45. Q: What governance and risk-management controls should surround a deep learning system?
A: Show answer
Governance should define the system's purpose, owner, authorized uses, affected stakeholders, risk classification, performance requirements, approval authority, and monitoring responsibilities. A practical control framework covers data provenance and privacy, model validation, security, bias assessment, documentation, version management, human oversight, incident response, and retirement. Responsibilities should be assigned across development, operations, risk management, and business ownership rather than left with a model developer alone. Evidence should be retained for important decisions, including reasons for deployment, limitations, test results, changes, and incidents. The National Institute of Standards and Technology's AI Risk Management Framework provides a recognized structure for considering trustworthiness and managing risk across the AI lifecycle. Applicable laws, sector requirements, contracts, and organizational policies must also be checked because a general framework does not replace binding requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Map a proposed AI system to its purpose, accountable owner, risk assessment, validation evidence, monitoring plan, and incident-response process."

Key point:
- Governance defines accountability and controls throughout the AI lifecycle.
- Risk frameworks support evaluation but do not replace applicable laws and sector-specific requirements.

Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- FDA — Good Machine Learning Practice for Medical Device Development
46. Q: How should human oversight be designed for deep learning applications with serious consequences?
A: Show answer
Human oversight should be designed around the risks of the intended task, the reliability of the model, and the ability of the reviewer to identify and correct errors. A reviewer needs sufficient context, relevant evidence, appropriate training, and the authority to reject a prediction or escalate an unusual case. The workflow should make uncertainty and limitations visible where possible, while avoiding the assumption that a human review automatically eliminates model risk. Overreliance on automated recommendations can create automation bias, especially when reviewers are overloaded or unable to inspect the underlying evidence. In some applications, the system should defer uncertain cases, require approval for high-impact actions, or provide a safe fallback when model confidence or input quality is inadequate. Oversight requirements should be tested as part of the actual operational process.

Study instruction:
- "Design a human-review workflow specifying escalation triggers, reviewer authority, required evidence, and handling of uncertain predictions."

Key point:
- Human oversight needs genuine authority, appropriate context, and a workable review process.
- High-impact actions may require explicit approval or a safe fallback when model reliability is uncertain.

- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- FDA — Good Machine Learning Practice for Medical Device Development
47. Q: How should an organization respond when a deployed model begins producing suspicious or unreliable outputs?
A: Show answer
The response should begin by assessing the scope and potential harm of the problem. The team should record the affected model version, time period, inputs or cases involved, observed failure patterns, and systems or decisions affected. It should investigate whether the cause relates to source data, preprocessing, distribution changes, software defects, unauthorized model changes, or inappropriate use. Depending on the risk, the organization may restrict the system, revert to a previously validated version, divert cases for human review, or temporarily disable automated actions. Corrections should be tested and authorized before full restoration. The team should document the root cause, affected outcomes, corrective measures, required notifications, and evidence that the issue has been resolved. Follow-up testing should establish whether related failure modes could recur elsewhere.

Study instruction:
- "Write an incident-response procedure covering detection, containment, investigation, correction, revalidation, communication, and lessons learned."

Key point:
- Model failures require documented investigation and proportionate containment.
- Restoring a system should follow validation and approval rather than an undocumented software change.

Source links:
- NIST — Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- FDA — Good Machine Learning Practice for Medical Device Development
48. Q: How can a team determine whether a proposed deep learning project is ready for deployment?
A: Show answer
Deployment readiness should be judged against predefined criteria rather than enthusiasm about model performance. The team should confirm that the problem and intended users are clearly defined, data use is authorized, training and evaluation datasets are appropriate, and the model meets relevant performance and robustness requirements. It should review subgroup outcomes, explainability needs, privacy and security, integration with operational systems, inference cost, latency, monitoring, human oversight, and failure handling. The deployed model should be identifiable by version, supported by documented approval, and accompanied by a plan for updates and rollback. Outstanding limitations should be recorded with owners and mitigation arrangements. A project that performs well in experiments may still be unready if operational accountability, monitoring, or user procedures have not been established. Readiness therefore depends on both technical evidence and organizational preparedness.

Study instruction:
- "Use a deployment-readiness checklist to evaluate performance, data governance, security, cost, monitoring, oversight, and rollback arrangements for a selected use case."

Key point:
- Deployment readiness includes technical, operational, and governance requirements.
- A strong experimental result is insufficient without monitoring, accountability, and failure-management arrangements.
