Table 9
The Future of Artificial Intelligence
1. Q: What is the central argument about the future of artificial intelligence (AI)?
A: Show answer
Artificial intelligence has the potential to transform transportation, healthcare, scientific research, business operations, public services, and environmental protection. It can improve the speed of analysis, identify patterns in complex information, automate repetitive tasks, and support decisions that would otherwise require substantial human effort. However, these benefits do not eliminate the risks of inaccurate outputs, discrimination, privacy violations, job displacement, cybersecurity threats, or misuse in warfare. The central challenge is therefore not simply to develop more powerful AI, but to ensure that its capabilities are deployed in ways that are safe, fair, accountable, and beneficial to society. Predictions about future AI should also be treated as uncertain rather than guaranteed outcomes. Historical forecasts must be reassessed against current evidence, actual deployment results, and emerging limitations.

Study instruction:
- Explain three potential benefits and three significant risks of AI, and distinguish technological capability from successful real-world implementation.

Key point:
- AI creates opportunities and risks that must be assessed together.
- Responsible deployment requires evidence, oversight, and accountability.

Source links:
- Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index Report
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
2. Q: How should forecasts about future AI capabilities and social impacts be evaluated?
A: Show answer
Evaluate forecasts by examining their assumptions, evidence, time horizon, intended use, and uncertainty. A forecast about a system performing well in a laboratory is not equivalent to evidence that it will operate safely at scale in real-world conditions. Predictions may also be affected by commercial incentives, political priorities, incomplete data, and assumptions about infrastructure or public acceptance. Separate demonstrated results from projections, expert opinions, and speculative scenarios. Record the date of each forecast, its measurable criteria, and the conditions under which it might prove incorrect. Compare earlier predictions with subsequent outcomes to identify recurring forecasting errors. For example, a historical projection about autonomous vehicles reaching mass adoption should not be treated as a current market fact without new evidence about deployment, safety, affordability, and regulation.

Study instruction:
- Select a historical AI forecast and compare its original assumptions with current evidence, identifying which claims remain supported and which require revision.

Key point:
- Forecasts are hypotheses about the future, not established facts.
- Decisions should rely on current evidence and explicit uncertainty.

Source links:
- Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index Report
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
3. Q: How did early autonomous-vehicle research contribute to modern AI-driven transportation?
A: Show answer
Early autonomous-vehicle research established that computers could use cameras, sensors, and programmed decision processes to guide vehicles without continuous manual control of every movement. Progress depended not only on better algorithms but also on computing power, sensor capabilities, navigation methods, testing environments, and investment. Historical projects demonstrated technical possibilities but also exposed practical limitations, including slow processing, unreliable perception, difficulty handling unexpected obstacles, and uncertainty about commercial viability. Modern systems benefit from improvements in hardware, machine learning, mapping, simulation, and integrated vehicle controls. Nevertheless, historical milestones do not establish that autonomous transportation is universally safe, affordable, or commercially sustainable. Each deployment must be assessed according to its operating conditions, intended functions, safety evidence, and remaining limitations.

Study instruction:
- Trace the development of autonomous vehicles from early research prototypes to modern systems and identify the technical and operational barriers that had to be addressed.

Key point:
- Technological progress depends on interacting advances in hardware, software, and operational design.
- Successful prototypes do not automatically establish safe commercial deployment.

Source links:
- NHTSA — Driver Assistance Technologies
- Politico Europe — Historical reporting on autonomous-vehicle development
4. Q: What technologies enable an autonomous vehicle to perceive and navigate its surroundings?
A: Show answer
Autonomous vehicles can combine cameras, radar, lidar, ultrasonic sensors, positioning systems, digital maps, onboard computing, and control software. Cameras provide visual information about lanes, signs, traffic lights, pedestrians, and vehicles. Radar helps estimate the distance and relative speed of nearby objects. Lidar measures distance using laser pulses, while ultrasonic sensors can support short-range obstacle detection. Positioning systems and maps provide additional information about location and road geometry. Sensor fusion combines observations from different sources, helping the system assess its environment more reliably than a single sensor might. The vehicle's control system then uses these estimates to plan or execute steering, acceleration, and braking. However, sensors can be obstructed, signals can be degraded, maps can become outdated, and algorithms can misinterpret unusual situations. Redundancy and failure detection are therefore critical design considerations.

Study instruction:
- Explain the function of at least four sensing or computing components and describe how sensor fusion can reduce, but not eliminate, perception errors.

Key point:
- Autonomous driving depends on coordinated perception, planning, and control.
- Multiple sensors improve coverage but do not guarantee correct interpretation.

Source links:
- NHTSA — Driver Assistance Technologies
- Stanford HAI — AI Technical Performance
5. Q: What are the six levels of driving automation, and why must they be distinguished carefully?
A: Show answer
The SAE automation framework distinguishes six levels, from Level 0 through Level 5. Level 0 involves no sustained driving automation, although warnings or momentary safety interventions may be provided. Level 1 provides assistance with either steering or acceleration and braking. Level 2 can assist with both steering and acceleration and braking, but the human driver remains responsible for monitoring the driving environment. Level 3 provides conditional driving automation, with a fallback-ready human expected to take over when requested. Level 4 can perform the driving task within its defined operational conditions without requiring a human driver to take over. Level 5 refers to automation capable of operating across all driving conditions covered by the full-automation definition. These distinctions matter because a feature marketed as advanced driving assistance does not necessarily make a vehicle self-driving. Users must understand who is responsible for monitoring, responding to failures, and controlling the vehicle under each system's approved conditions.

Study instruction:
- Memorize the differences between Levels 2, 3, 4, and 5, paying particular attention to driver responsibility and operating conditions.

Key point:
- Driver assistance and full driving automation are not interchangeable.
- Automation level determines important responsibilities and limitations.

Source links:
- NHTSA — Driver Assistance Technologies
6. Q: Why is autonomous driving difficult even when a vehicle performs well in ordinary traffic?
A: Show answer
Driving requires continuous interpretation of a changing environment in which other road users do not always behave predictably. A vehicle may encounter temporary roadworks, damaged traffic signs, unusual pedestrian movements, emergency vehicles, poor visibility, construction equipment, or unexpected obstacles. These situations are difficult because they may be rare, poorly represented in training data, or substantially different from the scenarios used during testing. A model that performs well under common conditions may still fail in an unusual but safety-critical situation. Adversarial conditions, sensor failure, software defects, and interactions between automated and human-driven vehicles can create additional risks. Reliable deployment therefore requires scenario-based testing, simulation, controlled real-world trials, incident monitoring, and procedures for safe disengagement or stopping when operational conditions exceed system capabilities.

Study instruction:
- Develop five unusual road scenarios and explain how perception, prediction, planning, and fallback procedures could fail in each one.

Key point:
- Common-condition performance does not establish universal safety.
- Rare failures and unpredictable interactions require explicit testing.

Source links:
- NHTSA — Driver Assistance Technologies
- Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index Report
7. Q: What is an operational design domain, and why is it important for autonomous vehicles?
A: Show answer
An operational design domain (ODD) defines the conditions under which an automated driving system is designed to operate. Depending on the system, these conditions may include road types, geographic boundaries, speed ranges, weather, lighting, traffic conditions, and other environmental limitations. A system that can operate safely within a mapped urban district under specified conditions should not automatically be assumed capable of handling every highway, rural road, or severe-weather situation. Manufacturers and operators need to identify the permitted operating domain, detect when conditions fall outside it, and define appropriate fallback behavior. This includes determining when the system should slow down, stop, request intervention, or discontinue operation. Clear ODD boundaries support safer deployment, more accurate public communication, regulatory evaluation, and the investigation of incidents.

Study instruction:
- Write an illustrative ODD for an autonomous shuttle and identify the conditions that would require it to suspend operation.

Key point:
- Safety claims must be tied to defined operating conditions.
- Systems need clear responses when those conditions are no longer satisfied.

Source links:
- NHTSA — Driver Assistance Technologies
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
8. Q: What are the main barriers to widespread adoption of autonomous vehicles?
A: Show answer
Adoption depends on more than technical performance. Important barriers include safety assurance, vehicle and sensor costs, maintenance, insurance, liability, infrastructure, regulation, accessibility, cybersecurity, and public confidence. Cities may need to consider road markings, construction management, charging or service facilities, and interactions between automated and human-driven traffic. Businesses must evaluate whether productivity gains exceed capital, operating, and transition costs. Regulators need evidence that vehicles comply with relevant safety requirements, while the public needs understandable information about limitations and responsibilities. Public perception should be measured rather than assumed: one survey or early pilot may not represent the attitudes of all users or regions. Adoption also varies by use case. A controlled industrial site or defined shuttle route may have different safety and economic characteristics from unrestricted private driving.

Study instruction:
- Rank the principal adoption barriers for a proposed autonomous-shuttle service and state what evidence would be needed before launch.

Key point:
- Deployment requires technical, economic, regulatory, and social readiness.
- Adoption forecasts must reflect the specific use case and operating environment.

Source links:
- NHTSA — Driver Assistance Technologies
- AAA — Historical survey on public attitudes toward self-driving vehicles
9. Q: How can the AI competition between the United States and China be assessed objectively?
A: Show answer
A meaningful comparison should examine several dimensions, including research output, advanced model performance, computing infrastructure, private and public investment, access to data, semiconductor supply, skilled workers, commercialization, and regulatory conditions. No single metric establishes overall leadership. Publication counts may indicate research activity but do not directly measure deployment quality or economic value. Investment figures can omit public funding mechanisms or differ in coverage. Data availability can support research, but privacy rules and restrictions on data use also affect what can responsibly be developed. Competition may be concentrated in some areas, while each country performs differently in others. Historical claims about future spending, rankings, or technological dominance should be treated as dated evidence and checked against current comparable statistics. The broader issue is how countries combine research capability, infrastructure, talent, trust, and governance to translate innovation into sustainable public and economic benefits.

Study instruction:
- Create a comparison using at least five indicators and document each indicator's definition, date, source, and limitations.

Key point:
- AI leadership is multidimensional and changes over time.
- Comparisons require current, comparable evidence rather than isolated statistics.

Source links:
- Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index Report
10. Q: Why does access to data matter for AI innovation, and what trade-offs does it create?
A: Show answer
Data supports AI training, testing, evaluation, and adaptation to particular environments. Large and diverse datasets may help systems recognize patterns across different circumstances, languages, populations, and applications. However, having more data does not automatically produce a better or safer model. Dataset quality, representativeness, accuracy, legal rights, consent, privacy, and the relevance of the data to the intended task all matter. Large collections may contain duplicates, errors, historical discrimination, or sensitive information. Broad access can improve research opportunities while increasing the risks of surveillance, misuse, and unauthorized disclosure. Organizations should identify a lawful basis for data use, minimize unnecessary collection, establish retention limits, restrict access, and evaluate whether the dataset reflects the people who will be affected. The goal is to obtain sufficient high-quality data while respecting individual rights and controlling downstream risks.

Study instruction:
- Assess a hypothetical AI dataset for coverage, accuracy, consent, sensitive information, representativeness, and appropriate retention.

Key point:
- Data quality and governance matter as much as data volume.
- Innovation must be balanced with privacy, fairness, and lawful use.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
11. Q: How can AI cause technological unemployment, and why is job automation different from job elimination?
A: Show answer
AI can automate particular tasks within a job without necessarily eliminating the entire occupation. A finance officer, for example, may use AI to summarize documents or identify unusual transactions while retaining responsibility for interpretation, approvals, stakeholder communication, and accountability. In other occupations, automation may reduce the number of workers required, change entry-level responsibilities, or eliminate particular roles. The final employment impact depends on technical capability, costs, work organization, regulation, customer preferences, and whether new tasks or services emerge. Exposure to AI is therefore not equivalent to inevitable unemployment. The International Labour Organization's research examines occupational exposure and the changing relationship between automation and task augmentation. Organizations should assess actual workflows rather than assuming that all tasks within an exposed occupation will disappear. Employment outcomes must be monitored after implementation.

Study instruction:
- Decompose one occupation into tasks and identify which tasks AI could automate, augment, or leave largely dependent on human judgment.

Key point:
- Task automation and elimination of an entire job are different outcomes.
- Actual employment effects depend on implementation and organizational change.

Source links:
- ILO — Generative AI and Jobs: A 2025 Update
- ILO — The Impact of Generative AI on Jobs, Productivity and Work Organization
12. Q: How should workers and organizations prepare for AI-related changes in employment?
A: Show answer
Preparation should begin with a realistic assessment of changing tasks and required skills. Organizations can identify workflows likely to change, provide training in AI-assisted work, strengthen data literacy, and help employees develop skills in judgment, communication, problem-solving, quality control, and domain expertise. Retraining should be linked to actual opportunities rather than offered as a generic response to uncertainty. Employers should involve workers in redesigning processes because employees often understand operational exceptions and customer needs that an implementation team may overlook. Governments and educational institutions can support lifelong learning, career transitions, employment services, and access to relevant training. Employers should also monitor effects on workload, job quality, career progression, and entry-level hiring. A responsible transition seeks to increase productivity without ignoring workers who face material disruption.

Study instruction:
- Design a transition plan for a department introducing AI, including skill assessment, training, employee consultation, and employment-impact monitoring.

Key point:
- Workforce preparation requires practical training and realistic transition pathways.
- Human involvement remains essential to successful organizational change.

Source links:
- ILO — Evidence on AI, Productivity and Work Organization
- UNESCO — Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
13. Q: How could AI contribute to economic inequality even if it increases overall productivity?
A: Show answer
Productivity gains do not guarantee that benefits will be distributed evenly. Companies that control valuable data, computing resources, infrastructure, intellectual property, or widely adopted AI services may capture a disproportionate share of the economic gains. Workers whose tasks are automated may experience reduced bargaining power or fewer entry-level opportunities, while highly skilled workers and business owners may benefit from increased productivity. Smaller organizations may struggle with implementation costs, while some communities may lack access to suitable training or digital infrastructure. Inequality may also arise when AI systems reproduce discrimination in hiring, lending, healthcare, or access to services. Evaluating the effects of AI therefore requires more than measuring aggregate productivity or investment. Policymakers and organizations should also examine wages, job quality, access to training, competition, affordability, and whether gains reach affected populations.

Study instruction:
- Identify three channels through which AI could increase inequality and propose a measurable indicator for each channel.

Key point:
- Aggregate economic gains can coexist with unequal distribution.
- Distributional effects should be measured rather than assumed.

Source links:
- ILO — The Impact of Generative AI on Jobs, Productivity and Work Organization
- Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index Report
14. Q: What are the advantages and limitations of policy proposals such as basic income and AI-related taxes?
A: Show answer
Basic income proposals aim to provide people with a predictable income floor, potentially helping them manage periods of unemployment or unstable earnings. Their effectiveness depends on benefit amounts, eligibility, financing, administrative design, effects on existing services, and broader labor-market conditions. AI-related taxes are another proposal intended to help society share in economic gains associated with automation. However, designing a tax specifically around AI can raise difficult questions about defining the taxable activity, measuring automation's contribution, avoiding unintended investment effects, and coordinating rules across jurisdictions. Neither proposal is a guaranteed solution to technological unemployment. Policymakers must compare these options with alternatives such as wage insurance, unemployment support, education, retraining, portable benefits, competition policy, and targeted assistance. The appropriate package depends on the type and scale of disruption, fiscal capacity, and evidence about actual outcomes.

Study instruction:
- Compare basic income, retraining assistance, and an AI-related tax in terms of objectives, financing, distributional effects, implementation difficulties, and evaluation measures.

Key point:
- AI transition policies involve trade-offs and depend on local circumstances.
- Proposed interventions should be evaluated against evidence and clearly defined objectives.

Source links:
- ILO — Evidence on AI, Employment and Work Organization
- Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index Report
15. Q: What risks arise when employers use AI to evaluate workers or predict employee behavior?
A: Show answer
AI systems can analyze performance records, attendance, communications, work patterns, or other employment-related data to generate predictions and recommendations. These tools may help identify operational bottlenecks or support workforce planning, but they can also create intrusive surveillance, inaccurate risk scores, discriminatory outcomes, and decisions that employees cannot meaningfully challenge. A prediction that an employee may resign, for example, is not proof that the person intends to leave. Historical data may reflect unequal treatment, outdated practices, or circumstances outside the employee's control. Employers should define the legitimate purpose, limit data collection, validate model performance, document limitations, test for disparate effects, and provide human review for consequential decisions. Employees should receive appropriate information about relevant monitoring and have a process to challenge material errors. Sensitive decisions should not be delegated to opaque predictions without meaningful accountability.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate a hypothetical employee-retention model and identify its intended purpose, data risks, possible bias, human-review requirements, and appeal process.

Key point:
- Predictive scores are not definitive evidence about an individual.
- Employment decisions require safeguards against misuse and unfair treatment.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
16. Q: What are the principal risks of using AI in military operations and autonomous weapons?
A: Show answer
AI can support image analysis, logistics, reconnaissance, threat assessment, and other military functions. However, systems that can identify, select, or engage targets with reduced human involvement raise serious questions about accountability, reliability, civilian protection, escalation, and compliance with international humanitarian law. A model can misclassify objects, fail in unfamiliar conditions, or respond unpredictably to manipulated inputs. Human operators may also become over-reliant on recommendations that appear precise but remain uncertain. Responsible military use requires appropriate legal review, rigorous testing, clearly defined operational limits, command responsibility, and meaningful human judgment and control. Human supervision should be substantive rather than merely a nominal approval step. International discussions about autonomous weapons continue to evolve, so descriptions of the legal and diplomatic position must be based on current official material instead of outdated assumptions about whether a particular prohibition or treaty has already been adopted.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish AI-supported military decision-making from a weapon system that can select and engage targets autonomously, and identify the human responsibilities in each case.

Key point:
- Military AI creates high-consequence legal, ethical, and operational risks.
- Meaningful human judgment and accountability are central safeguards.

Source links:
- United Nations — Secretary-General's Statement on Lethal Autonomous Weapons
- UNIDIR — Human-Machine Interfaces in Autonomous Weapon Systems
17. Q: How can AI intensify misinformation, deepfakes, and information warfare?
A: Show answer
AI can generate convincing text, images, audio, and video at scale, lowering the effort required to produce deceptive material. Malicious actors may impersonate public figures, fabricate evidence, create fraudulent voice messages, or tailor misleading content to particular audiences. Automated systems may also assist with translation, distribution, and rapid adaptation of messages across platforms. The resulting risk is not limited to whether individual content items are false; high-volume deception can undermine confidence in authentic information and institutions. Responses should combine provenance information where available, platform policies, independent verification, media literacy, secure communication practices, and effective reporting channels. Organizations should establish verification procedures for high-risk requests, especially financial transfers, sensitive disclosures, and emergency instructions. Detection tools can help but should not be treated as infallible because both genuine content and synthetic content can be misclassified.

Study instruction:
- Design a verification procedure for an apparently authentic executive voice message requesting an urgent bank transfer.

Key point:
- Synthetic content can increase the scale and plausibility of deception.
- Verification should rely on independent evidence, not appearance alone.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — Generative AI Risk Management Profile
18. Q: How can AI improve drug discovery and pharmaceutical research?
A: Show answer
AI can help researchers analyze biological data, identify patterns related to disease, propose candidate molecules, estimate molecular properties, prioritize experiments, and explore potential treatment mechanisms. It may also help organize scientific literature or identify relationships that are difficult to detect manually. These applications can reduce the time spent on some research tasks and help teams decide which hypotheses to investigate. However, a promising computational result does not establish that a compound is safe or effective in humans. Predictions depend on the quality and relevance of training data, the assumptions of the model, and the biological system being studied. Experimental work, toxicology, clinical trials, manufacturing controls, and regulatory evaluation remain essential. AI can improve the research process, but its contribution must be measured through validated results rather than assuming that more predictions automatically produce more successful medicines.

Study instruction:
- Map a drug-development workflow and identify where AI can support candidate selection, laboratory testing, clinical evidence analysis, and quality assessment.

Key point:
- AI can accelerate selected research tasks and prioritize experiments.
- Computational predictions do not replace scientific and clinical validation.

Source links:
- FDA — Guiding Principles of Good AI Practice in Drug Development
- KFF Health News — Historical reporting on AI in drug discovery and healthcare
19. Q: How should AI-generated scientific hypotheses be validated before they influence medical decisions?
A: Show answer
Validation begins with a clearly defined research question and an explicit description of how the model will be used. Researchers should examine the provenance and suitability of the data, prevent data leakage between training and evaluation sets, compare results with suitable baselines, and report uncertainty and limitations. A model that performs well on historical data may fail when tested in different hospitals, populations, laboratories, or clinical conditions. Where appropriate, findings should be replicated independently and tested prospectively. For medical applications, evidence should address safety, effectiveness, reproducibility, and the consequences of incorrect predictions. Developers and researchers must document model versions, data preparation, evaluation methods, exclusions, and deviations from the original protocol. Regulatory requirements depend on the intended use and jurisdiction; an AI tool used to support research does not automatically have the evidence needed for clinical decision-making or regulatory submission.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a validation checklist covering data leakage, external testing, reproducibility, uncertainty, intended use, and consequences of false predictions.

Key point:
- Strong internal test results do not prove general clinical validity.
- Validation must match the real-world purpose and risk of the system.

Source links:
- FDA — Considerations for AI Supporting Regulatory Decision-Making
- FDA — Good AI Practice in Drug Development
20. Q: What ethical principles should guide AI applications in healthcare?
A: Show answer
Healthcare AI should be designed and used to promote patient well-being while respecting human rights, privacy, fairness, safety, and accountability. Systems may support diagnosis, clinical documentation, research, treatment planning, and public-health surveillance, but they can also produce inaccurate recommendations, perform poorly for underrepresented groups, or encourage over-reliance by healthcare professionals. Organizations should assess the quality and representativeness of the data, establish appropriate clinical validation, protect sensitive health information, and clearly define when professional review is required. Patients and healthcare workers should understand the relevant role and limitations of the system. Monitoring should continue after deployment because data, clinical practice, and model performance may change. AI should support responsible clinical judgment rather than replacing professional accountability without sufficient evidence and authorization.

Study instruction:
- Apply a healthcare AI ethics checklist to one proposed clinical tool and identify the safety, privacy, fairness, and accountability controls needed before deployment.

Key point:
- Healthcare AI must be evaluated against patient safety and human rights.
- Clinical oversight and post-deployment monitoring remain necessary.

Source links:
- WHO — Ethics and Governance of AI for Health: Guidance on Large Multimodal Models
- WHO — Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
21. Q: What privacy concerns arise when AI-powered speakers and digital assistants process voice recordings?
A: Show answer
Voice-enabled systems may process recordings to recognize commands, generate responses, improve services, or detect errors. Privacy concerns arise when users do not understand when recording occurs, how long information is retained, whether human reviewers can access it, or whether data may be used for additional purposes. Voice data can contain conversations about health, finances, relationships, or other sensitive matters. Organizations should disclose relevant practices clearly, minimize collection, secure stored recordings, restrict staff access, define retention periods, and provide appropriate user controls. They should also assess whether third-party processors receive information and how incidents will be handled. A technically capable assistant is not automatically privacy-preserving. Users should have enough information to understand material data practices, and organizations must comply with applicable privacy and consumer-protection requirements.

Study instruction:
- Review a hypothetical voice-assistant privacy notice and identify gaps concerning consent, retention, human review, third-party access, and deletion.

Key point:
- Voice processing may expose sensitive information beyond the immediate command.
- Privacy protection requires transparent policies and practical technical controls.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
22. Q: What are the risks of using facial recognition and other AI systems in law enforcement?
A: Show answer
Facial recognition and related systems may help compare images, search records, or prioritize investigative leads. However, errors can arise from poor image quality, mismatched databases, unequal performance across demographic groups, or misleading contextual information. A false match may cause a person to be investigated or subjected to other serious consequences. The use of such systems can also raise concerns about mass surveillance, privacy, freedom of expression, and the ability to challenge government decisions. Organizations should establish a lawful and clearly defined purpose, assess necessity and proportionality, test performance on relevant populations, document limitations, control access, and retain an auditable record of use. AI-generated matches should not automatically be treated as proof of identity or guilt. Human review must involve independent evidence and meaningful scrutiny rather than merely accepting the algorithm's recommendation.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a hypothetical facial-recognition match and identify the additional evidence, safeguards, review procedures, and legal checks needed before acting on it.

Key point:
- A matching score is not conclusive proof of identity or wrongdoing.
- High-impact surveillance requires strong safeguards and accountability.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
23. Q: What does algorithmic accountability mean in practice?
A: Show answer
Algorithmic accountability means that an organization can explain why an AI system is used, who authorized it, how it was evaluated, what risks were identified, and how affected people can obtain review or challenge errors. Practical measures may include an inventory of AI systems, documented intended uses, data and model documentation, impact assessments, performance testing, named risk owners, approval controls, incident reporting, and periodic reassessment. Where an AI system affects employment, access to services, healthcare, or other important outcomes, the organization should establish a proportionate process for human intervention and correction. Accountability is not achieved merely by publishing a high-level ethical statement or providing a technical explanation that ordinary users cannot understand. It requires enforceable responsibilities, evidence of implementation, and a process for addressing harm. Specific legal obligations depend on the jurisdiction and application.

Study instruction:
- Draft an accountability record for a proposed AI system, including its purpose, owner, affected users, risk assessment, evidence, review process, and incident escalation route.

Key point:
- Accountability requires identifiable responsibilities and documented decisions.
- Affected people need appropriate ways to challenge harmful errors.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
24. Q: What is artificial general intelligence, and how does it differ from today's specialized AI systems?
A: Show answer
Artificial general intelligence (AGI) is commonly used to describe a hypothetical AI system with broad capabilities across a wide variety of tasks, rather than performance limited to a particular application. Specialized AI systems may perform exceptionally well at image classification, speech recognition, language generation, or scientific prediction without possessing general human-like competence. Broader intelligence would require capabilities such as adapting to unfamiliar tasks, transferring knowledge across domains, reasoning under uncertainty, learning efficiently, and handling changing circumstances. There is no universally agreed operational test that conclusively establishes AGI, and definitions differ across researchers and organizations. Current capability improvements should therefore not automatically be interpreted as proof that AGI has been achieved. Claims about when AGI will arrive remain uncertain and should be assessed according to measurable capabilities, evaluation methods, and limitations rather than confident predictions alone.

Study instruction:
- Compare a specialized AI system with a hypothetical general-purpose system and identify which capabilities would need to be demonstrated to support an AGI claim.

Key point:
- Strong performance in a narrow task does not establish general intelligence.
- AGI definitions and timelines remain uncertain and contested.

Source links:
- Stanford HAI — AI Technical Performance
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
25. Q: What technical challenges must AI research address to achieve more general and reliable intelligence?
A: Show answer
Important challenges include reliable reasoning, transfer learning, causal understanding, efficient learning from limited examples, long-term planning, memory, robust perception, and adaptation to unfamiliar conditions. Many systems can identify patterns in training data without understanding whether a relationship is causal or whether it will remain valid when circumstances change. Some models require extensive computing resources or large datasets, while others produce inconsistent answers when prompts or environmental conditions change. Robust general-purpose intelligence would also need effective ways to recognize uncertainty, learn from mistakes, use tools safely, and avoid confidently producing unsupported conclusions. Progress in one benchmark does not demonstrate that all these challenges have been solved. Research should distinguish measured advances from speculation about future capabilities and evaluate systems on tasks that test robustness, adaptation, reliability, and real-world usefulness.

Study instruction:
- Choose three technical challenges and explain how each could affect a system operating outside its training environment.

Key point:
- Pattern recognition alone does not guarantee robust reasoning or adaptation.
- General capability requires evidence across diverse, demanding conditions.

Source links:
- Stanford HAI — AI Technical Performance
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
26. Q: How should AI capabilities be measured, and why can benchmark results be misleading?
A: Show answer
Benchmarks provide standardized ways to compare systems on defined tasks, but their conclusions depend on the quality of the test, the relevance of the task, and the conditions under which it is administered. A model may perform well on a benchmark because the test resembles its training data, while performing less reliably on unfamiliar inputs or in high-stakes workflows. Benchmark scores can also become less informative when they saturate, when evaluation data leak into training, or when a task does not represent real-world constraints. A sound evaluation program combines appropriate benchmarks with independent testing, adversarial testing, human assessment, operational monitoring, and analysis of failure cases. For consequential applications, evaluators should consider error severity, fairness, robustness, calibration, privacy, cybersecurity, and whether performance remains acceptable after deployment. Capability testing and responsible-use testing should be treated as complementary activities.

Study instruction:
- Design an evaluation plan for an AI assistant that includes performance metrics, independent test data, failure analysis, and safeguards for real-world use.

Key point:
- A benchmark measures a defined capability, not every aspect of reliability.
- Operational and risk-based testing complement standard performance scores.

Source links:
- Stanford HAI — AI Technical Performance
- NIST — Generative AI Risk Management Profile
27. Q: How can AI support social good in public health, conservation, and environmental protection?
A: Show answer
AI can help researchers analyze disease patterns, detect environmental changes, process satellite imagery, identify signs of illegal logging, monitor wildlife, and prioritize inspections or interventions. In public health, pattern detection may help authorities investigate outbreaks or allocate resources, provided data quality and privacy are addressed. In conservation, image or audio classification can help identify threats across large geographic areas. These applications can improve the efficiency of human teams, but they do not automatically establish the accuracy of a diagnosis, the cause of an environmental event, or the appropriate response. Poor data, false positives, unequal geographic coverage, and gaps in local knowledge can produce harmful decisions. Effective projects should involve domain experts and affected communities, define measurable outcomes, validate predictions, and ensure there are resources and procedures to act on reliable findings.

Study instruction:
- Design a conservation or public-health AI project with a defined outcome, data-quality controls, an evaluation method, and a human-led response process.

Key point:
- AI can extend the scale and speed of social-impact work.
- Benefits depend on validation, local context, and effective human action.

Source links:
- McKinsey — Applying Artificial Intelligence for Social Good
- WHO — Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence for Health
28. Q: What environmental and energy considerations arise from the expansion of AI?
A: Show answer
AI systems require computing infrastructure, data storage, networking, and electricity. Their environmental effects may include energy consumption, water use for some cooling systems, hardware manufacturing, electronic waste, and the resource requirements of supporting infrastructure. The scale of these effects depends on model size, usage volume, data-center efficiency, electricity sources, cooling technology, and the useful work produced by the system. AI may also support energy optimization, scientific research, and environmental monitoring, so a complete assessment should consider both operational costs and potential benefits. Organizations should not assume that efficiency improvements automatically reduce total consumption because increased use may offset savings. Environmental assessments should measure relevant resource use and consider alternatives with lower computational requirements. The International Energy Agency's recent analysis of AI and energy provides a useful basis for understanding the evolving relationship between data-center demand and energy systems.

Study instruction:
- Compare two AI implementation options by estimating their computing requirements, energy use, expected benefit, and possible lower-resource alternatives.

Key point:
- AI's environmental impact includes infrastructure and lifecycle resource use.
- Greater efficiency does not automatically guarantee lower total consumption.

Source links:
- International Energy Agency — Key Questions on Energy and AI
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
29. Q: How can organizations apply an AI risk-management framework before and after deployment?
A: Show answer
A practical risk-management process begins by identifying the purpose of the system, the people affected, the decisions it influences, and the consequences of failure. Organizations should then assess data suitability, performance, fairness, privacy, security, transparency, human oversight, and operational dependencies. Risks should be prioritized according to their likelihood, severity, exposure, and the organization's capacity to respond. Controls may include access restrictions, independent evaluation, human approval, fallback procedures, documentation, and incident reporting. Before deployment, responsible owners should determine whether residual risks are acceptable and obtain the required approvals. After deployment, monitoring should track changing performance, user behavior, incidents, and changes in the operating environment. NIST's AI Risk Management Framework provides a structured approach built around Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage. Its use does not itself establish legal compliance; applicable law and organizational obligations must still be checked.

Study instruction:
- Apply the Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage functions to a proposed AI system and identify evidence required at each stage.

Key point:
- AI governance must cover the full lifecycle, not only initial approval.
- Risk-management frameworks support but do not replace legal compliance.

Source links:
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
- NIST — Generative AI Risk Management Profile
30. Q: How can bias enter an AI system, and what can be done to reduce unfair outcomes?
A: Show answer
Bias can arise from unrepresentative training data, historical discrimination, inaccurate labels, unsuitable model objectives, missing context, or the way users interpret and act on predictions. A system trained on past hiring decisions, for example, may reproduce previous inequalities rather than identify the most qualified candidates. Bias can also arise after deployment if a model performs differently across populations or if some groups experience more severe consequences from the same type of error. Mitigation begins by identifying affected groups and the relevant definition of fairness for the application. Evaluation should examine appropriate subgroup performance and error rates, involve domain experts, and consider the consequences of false positives and false negatives. Data adjustments, model changes, process controls, or limits on use may be necessary. Fairness cannot be established by a single metric alone; the choice of measures should reflect the context, legal obligations, and human consequences.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a hypothetical AI hiring model and propose tests for data representation, subgroup performance, disparate impact, and appeal mechanisms.

Key point:
- Biased data and decisions can produce unfair outcomes even when a model appears accurate overall.
- Fairness requires context-specific testing and ongoing monitoring.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
31. Q: Why is meaningful human oversight necessary when AI supports important decisions?
A: Show answer
Human oversight provides a mechanism for reviewing outputs, considering information the model may not capture, detecting unusual cases, and taking responsibility for consequential decisions. However, merely placing a person in the approval process does not guarantee effective oversight. Reviewers may lack relevant expertise, face excessive workloads, or automatically accept recommendations that appear authoritative. Effective oversight requires access to supporting evidence, training in model limitations, clear decision rights, sufficient review time, and authority to reject or escalate recommendations. High-impact decisions should include documented reasons and appropriate avenues for correction or appeal. Organizations should also monitor whether reviewers genuinely challenge questionable outputs instead of treating approval as a routine formality. The appropriate level of oversight depends on the consequence of error, the system's reliability, and the reversibility of the decision.

Study instruction:
- Design a human-review procedure for an AI system that recommends high-impact decisions and explain how you would test whether reviewers intervene appropriately.

Key point:
- Human oversight must be informed, independent, and actionable.
- Accountability cannot be transferred to an algorithm.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
32. Q: What security risks accompany AI systems, and how can organizations prepare for misuse or failure?
A: Show answer
AI systems can be exposed to unauthorized access, manipulated inputs, malicious instructions, sensitive-data leakage, compromised dependencies, and attacks on training or retrieval data. Generative AI can also help malicious users create deceptive content or automate certain harmful activities. Security controls should reflect the system's purpose, connected applications, access privileges, and potential consequences of misuse. Useful measures include strong identity and access management, data minimization, separation of sensitive systems, secure development, adversarial testing, output validation, logging, and incident-response procedures. Organizations should also limit the actions an AI-enabled tool can perform without approval, particularly when it can send messages, change records, initiate transactions, or access confidential information. Security assessments need to continue after launch because models, integrations, users, and attack methods may change over time.

Study instruction:
- Create a threat model for an AI assistant connected to internal documents and business applications, identifying attack paths and preventive, detective, and corrective controls.

Key point:
- AI risks extend beyond inaccurate answers to system access and misuse.
- Least privilege, testing, monitoring, and incident response reduce exposure.

Source links:
- NIST — Generative AI Risk Management Profile
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
33. Q: How should an organization decide whether a proposed AI project is worth implementing?
A: Show answer
The organization should begin with a specific business or public-service problem rather than adopting AI merely because the technology is available. It should define the expected benefit, intended users, current baseline, measurable success criteria, implementation cost, required data, operating constraints, and consequences of failure. A suitable evaluation compares AI with simpler alternatives such as process redesign, conventional automation, or improved data quality. The organization should test a limited pilot against the baseline, evaluate performance across relevant scenarios, and calculate total costs that may include infrastructure, integration, training, supervision, security, and maintenance. It should also assess fairness, privacy, legal requirements, stakeholder impact, and exit options. Deployment should proceed only when the evidence supports the intended use and responsible owners accept the residual risk. A system that works technically may still be economically unjustified or operationally inappropriate.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an AI business case containing a baseline, measurable outcomes, alternatives, total cost, risk assessment, pilot design, and explicit deployment criteria.

Key point:
- Start with a defined problem and measurable value.
- Technical feasibility alone does not justify deployment.

Source links:
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
- Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index Report
34. Q: What should an autonomous-vehicle operator do when a road sign is changed or environmental conditions become unsafe?
A: Show answer
The operator should ensure that the vehicle follows approved procedures for detecting uncertainty or conditions outside its operating domain. Depending on the system, the response may include reducing speed, increasing following distance, requesting remote assistance, moving to a safe stopping location, or suspending service. The precise response should be established through safety engineering and validated for the vehicle's operating conditions rather than improvised during an incident. The operator should preserve relevant system logs, sensor observations, environmental information, and human interventions for investigation. If a potentially hazardous design weakness is identified, affected operations may need to be restricted while the issue is assessed. Restarting service should depend on documented corrective action and appropriate authorization. An unusual road sign or unexpected environment should be treated as a safety-relevant event, not automatically classified as a harmless anomaly.

Study instruction:
- Write an incident-response sequence for an autonomous shuttle encountering an unexpected road closure, including safe fallback, escalation, evidence preservation, and return-to-service approval.

Key point:
- Unexpected conditions require validated fallback behavior.
- Incident closure requires evidence and authorized corrective action.

Source links:
- NHTSA — Driver Assistance Technologies
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
35. Q: How should a business respond when an AI implementation improves productivity but reduces demand for certain roles?
A: Show answer
Management should examine both the operational gain and the effects on employees. It should verify that productivity improvements are real, durable, and not offset by rework, error correction, additional supervision, or deteriorating service quality. The organization should identify which tasks have changed, which roles remain necessary, and what new responsibilities may emerge. Options can include redeployment, retraining, redesigned roles, reduced reliance on temporary hiring, and transition support where job losses cannot be avoided. Decisions should account for contractual obligations, employment law, collective arrangements where applicable, and the organization's communication responsibilities. Management should also review whether the implementation concentrates benefits among a few employees or creates excessive workloads for others. A responsible response uses measured operational evidence, transparent criteria, and a documented process for managing workforce impacts.

Study instruction:
- Analyze an AI automation pilot using productivity, quality, total cost, workload, training, and employment measures before proposing a workforce decision.

Key point:
- Productivity should be measured alongside quality and workforce outcomes.
- Employment decisions require transparent criteria and appropriate safeguards.

Source links:
- ILO — Evidence on AI, Productivity and Work Organization
- ILO — Generative AI and Jobs: A 2025 Update
36. Q: What should a hospital do if an AI clinical-support system gives a recommendation that conflicts with a clinician's judgment?
A: Show answer
The hospital should follow a predefined clinical escalation process. The clinician should review the relevant patient information, examine the system's stated purpose and limitations, and determine whether additional evidence or specialist consultation is needed. The AI recommendation should not override appropriate professional judgment merely because it appears precise or is generated automatically. The organization should document material disagreements, investigate whether they arise from data errors, model limitations, changes in patient conditions, or differences in interpretation, and escalate possible safety issues. If the system may be producing systematically unreliable recommendations, its use may need to be restricted while the issue is investigated. Any corrective action should be supported by clinical expertise, technical assessment, and applicable regulatory requirements. Patient safety and the quality of evidence should take priority over automatic acceptance of model output.

Study instruction:
- Develop a clinical escalation checklist for conflicting AI recommendations, including patient assessment, evidence review, documentation, safety reporting, and possible restrictions on use.

Key point:
- Clinical AI is decision support, not automatic proof of the correct treatment.
- Material disagreements require professional judgment and documented review.

Source links:
- WHO — Ethics and Governance of AI for Health: Guidance on Large Multimodal Models
- FDA — Guiding Principles of Good AI Practice in Drug Development
37. Q: How should a public authority assess a proposal to use AI for identifying individuals from public-space camera footage?
A: Show answer
The authority should first determine whether the proposed use has a lawful and clearly defined purpose, whether it is necessary and proportionate, and whether less intrusive alternatives could achieve the same objective. It should assess the system's accuracy, demographic performance, potential for false matches, data sources, retention, access controls, and consequences for affected individuals. Independent evaluation and legal review should precede deployment, particularly if the system could influence investigations or other consequential decisions. Procedures must specify how human reviewers verify matches, how affected people can challenge errors where appropriate, and how misuse or unauthorized access is investigated. The authority should also consider community impact, public transparency, and mechanisms for independent oversight. If the system cannot meet the required legal, technical, and human-rights safeguards, the proposal should be redesigned or rejected.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an impact assessment for a proposed facial-recognition deployment, including necessity, proportionality, error risks, oversight, transparency, and complaint procedures.

Key point:
- Public-sector deployment requires legal justification and meaningful safeguards.
- High-impact errors can harm individuals even when the system is technically sophisticated.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
38. Q: How can organizations prevent outdated statistics and historical AI predictions from misleading decision-makers?
A: Show answer
Every material statistic should be linked to its source, publication date, measurement period, geographic coverage, definition, and limitations. Forecasts should be labeled as projections and separated from actual results. When a source refers to market estimates, public attitudes, investment, or technological capabilities from an earlier period, the organization should not silently present those figures as current. Researchers should seek updated official or high-quality evidence, check whether methodologies have changed, and avoid comparing numbers that use different definitions. Historical information remains useful for understanding the development of an idea, but it should not substitute for current market conditions, legal rules, or technical evaluations. Reports should also make uncertainty visible and document any assumptions that materially influence a conclusion.

Study instruction:
- Audit a historical AI report and classify its claims as current evidence, historical observation, forecast, opinion, or unsupported inference.

Key point:
- Dates and definitions are essential to interpreting quantitative claims.
- Historical predictions should be reassessed before guiding current decisions.

Source links:
- Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index Report
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
39. Q: How should governments and organizations balance AI innovation with safety, rights, and regulation?
A: Show answer
A balanced approach encourages beneficial innovation while applying safeguards proportionate to the potential consequences of failure. Low-risk applications may require relatively light controls, whereas systems affecting health, employment, public safety, or fundamental rights may require stronger validation, documentation, human oversight, and independent review. Governance should address the complete lifecycle from design and data collection through deployment, monitoring, modification, and retirement. Rules should be understandable and enforceable, with responsibilities assigned to developers, deployers, operators, and relevant authorities. International coordination can help address cross-border risks and reduce inconsistent expectations, while local law determines many concrete obligations. Regulation should be informed by evidence and should be reviewed as technology and use cases evolve. The goal is neither unrestricted deployment nor automatic rejection of AI, but responsible development that provides measurable value within acceptable legal, ethical, and safety boundaries.

Study instruction:
- Compare a low-risk AI application with a high-impact application and propose proportionate requirements for testing, documentation, oversight, and incident reporting.

Key point:
- Governance should be proportionate to risk and impact.
- Innovation and accountability must be designed to operate together.

Source links:
- UNESCO — Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
40. Q: What practical principles should guide a responsible long-term strategy for AI?
A: Show answer
A responsible AI strategy should begin with clear objectives and an honest assessment of capability, uncertainty, and potential harm. It should prioritize applications where evidence indicates meaningful benefit, protect sensitive information, test systems independently, and define human responsibilities for consequential decisions. Organizations should build evaluation and monitoring into the implementation plan, rather than treating them as activities performed only before launch. Workers and affected communities should have suitable opportunities to provide feedback, while incidents and errors should lead to documented corrective action. Decision-makers should distinguish established results from forecasts, avoid assuming that automation guarantees savings, and assess the distribution of benefits and burdens. Finally, governance should remain adaptable: systems, risks, laws, and evidence change, so policies and controls must be reviewed periodically. Long-term success depends on technical progress combined with reliable institutions, human judgment, and demonstrable public or organizational value.

Study instruction:
- Create a one-page AI strategy containing priority use cases, measurable benefits, risk ownership, validation requirements, human oversight, monitoring indicators, and review dates.

Key point:
- Responsible AI requires evidence-based deployment and continuous oversight.
- Sustainable benefits depend on people, governance, and technology working together.

Source links:
- Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index Report
- NIST — AI Risk Management Framework
